| 2026-08-09 | **HTTPS options — one certificate for every name the site answers to, an override for the check we get wrong, and a certificate you already have** (`ssl_service.names_for`/`check_names`/`certbot_domain_flags`/`valid_name`, `SslIn.force`, new `cert_install_service`) | Ploi's SSL screen has seven items; three now exist here. **(1) The certificate covered ONE domain.** Nearly every real site is also served at `www.`, so half the visitors got a browser security warning on a site whose owner had been told HTTPS was on — worse than no certificate, because it looks handled. Ploi asks for the extra names in a comma-separated box; ours already knows them, because they are the site's aliases, and asking again would be a worse version of something already solved. **The rule that makes it safe is that a stale alias is EXCLUDED, not fatal**: Let's Encrypt validates every name on a request and fails the whole thing if one cannot be reached, so a leftover domain the customer stopped using would otherwise block HTTPS entirely — and certbot's error names the alias without saying the rest were fine. What is excluded is reported, never dropped quietly. The site's own domain stays fatal, because it names the certificate. That feature now leans on an invariant the aliases endpoint already had — the vhost is written BEFORE the row is saved, so every alias in the row really is in `server_name`, which is what certbot's nginx plugin needs — so it gained a test that fails if a refactor ever saves the row first. **(2) "Request anyway", which is Ploi's skip-DNS-verification, exists because our readiness check is WRONG in one very common case**: a site behind Cloudflare's proxy or any CDN resolves to their addresses by design, so comparing addresses says "points somewhere else" while an HTTP request still arrives here and the certificate issues perfectly well. Refusing those customers outright was worse than letting them decide. It is offered only where it can apply — a domain that resolves NOWHERE cannot be validated by anyone, so the button would only spend one of the five attempts allowed each week — and the refusal now names the innocent cause, since "not to this server" on its own sends somebody to break a working setup. **(3) Install a certificate you already have**, for a wildcard, an organisation-validated certificate, or a Cloudflare origin certificate. **The private key is the most sensitive thing a customer will ever paste into ServerAlly**, so it never touches a command line: it goes over SFTP, and the shell only handles the permissions, the config edit and the reload. It is not stored and not logged. **Everything decidable is decided before the server is touched at all** — a key that does not belong to its certificate makes nginx refuse to start, which takes down every site on the machine, and a certificate for the wrong domain warns every visitor. Wildcard matching is deliberately no more generous than a browser's (`*.shop.com` covers `www.` and not the bare name or two labels deep), because being generous means installing something browsers then reject. A passphrase-protected key is refused with the command to unlock it, since nginx cannot be asked for one at boot and simply never starts. **Two bugs found by RUNNING it against real nginx, neither visible by reading.** The insertion matched both `listen 80;` and `listen [::]:80;`, so an ordinary vhost got a **duplicate 443 listener and nginx refused to start** — it is once per BLOCK now, proven by a test that executes the real script. And replacing a certificate overwrites the live files before the config is known to be good, so a refused config left the running site pointing at a file swapped underneath it; the previous pair is now kept and put back. Also carries the pre-existing-outage rule the WordPress switches learned: a site that was already down keeps the certificate and is told so, rather than having the change blamed and reverted. Proven in containers against real nginx across four cases — a plain site gaining HTTPS and serving its real page, a replacement repointing every block and serving the NEW certificate, a refused configuration leaving the file byte-identical with no leftovers and the site still up, and an already-broken site keeping the certificate. 46 + 27 tests; **all 12 mutations killed**, including the one that puts the duplicate-listener bug back. One process note worth keeping: a mutation run killed by a timeout left the source MUTATED, and the next run then measured against a broken baseline — the restore has to survive being interrupted, not only being finished. **Honest gap: the paste form has not been driven in a browser, because every site on the account is on a CyberPanel server and HTTPS is deliberately absent there** — a panel owns its own certificates and rewrites the vhost on its own schedule, which the endpoint refuses by name as well. Remaining from Ploi's SSL screen: ZeroSSL, a signing request, DNS-provider validation, HTTP/3. |
| 2026-08-10 | **The rest of Ploi's per-site screens — the artisan grid, the customer's own commands, wp-config, WP-CLI, notes and grouping; and a shipped bug the new sweep caught** (`laravel_service.ACTIONS`/`check_custom`, `wp_config_service`, `wordpress_service.check_wp_command`, migration 062, `test_no_unbound_names` redefinition sweep) | Asked what remained in Ploi and to build it. Checking first found something worse than a missing feature: **yesterday's Laravel work had added a second `read()` to `laravel_service`, which silently replaced the first** — Python keeps the later definition, so three callers of the original were raising TypeError and the whole Laravel application section was broken. Nothing at import time or in review says a word about this. So the fix came with a **sweep that fails if any module defines the same top-level name twice**, and it earned its keep twice more within the hour: it caught `redact_secrets` used in a function that never imported it, and a **second `_wp_context`** I was about to add beside the one the security switches already use — which would have shadowed mine and handed my endpoints a two-tuple where they unpacked three. **What shipped.** Ploi's artisan grid is 24 commands in 11 groups; we had 7. The gap was never the dangerous half — we offer `migrate` and they do not — it was the everyday one, so the map went to 17 writes plus the 6 reads, grouped, each explained without the word artisan. **Four of them are asked about and the rest are not**, deliberately: `queue:flush` destroys the record of failed work, `queue:retry all` re-runs jobs that may have half-succeeded (the classic outcome is a customer charged twice), `schedule:run` fires real work outside its schedule, `migrate` can drop a column. Confirming a cache clear as well would teach people to click through the question. **Custom commands** are bounded by a refusal list rather than an allow-list, because an application's own commands are the whole reason artisan exists and no fixed list could know them; `db:wipe`, `migrate:fresh` and friends are **refused rather than confirmed**, since a confirmation is a thing people click and the cost is the customer's entire dataset. Two bugs there, both mine, both caught by running it: quoting the command as one string handed artisan a single argument literally named `app:send --dry` (**the daemons `ExecStart` mistake, inverted** — it is quoted word by word now, asserted the way a shell would read it), and normalising whitespace BEFORE the metacharacter check meant the newline rule could never fire. **wp-config.php** gets the `.env` editor's discipline plus the one that matters more here: the new file is parsed by real `php -l` BEFORE the live one is replaced, because replacing first leaves a window where every visitor gets a blank page. It also warns about a `define()` below the "stop editing" line — WordPress loads the rest of itself there and never reads what follows, so the setting is plainly in the file and does nothing, which is the most confusing outcome this screen can produce. **WP-CLI** is the same shape as the artisan console, refusing `db`, `eval` and `shell`. **Notes and tags** (migration 062) are the only two fields on a site that are purely the customer's own — nothing derives them, so nothing can recover them — and tags dedupe case-insensitively and are refused rather than truncated, because a tag cut in half stops matching the ones on other sites, which is the entire point of grouping. Mutation-tested throughout: 10 mutations on wp-config all killed, and the survivor is worth recording — the empty-file guard is redundant against the `<?php` check, so it exists for its MESSAGE, and the test now asserts that rather than merely that it raises. Also fixed a curl stub of my own that consumed `-o` with a `for` loop, silently wrote every body to /dev/null, and made every run look like a broken site. **Ploi's "Webhook URLs" turned out to be already done** — the deploy emits `deploy.started`/`deploy.finished` through the endpoint system that signs, retries and logs, rather than a raw POST nobody can verify. **Deliberately still absent: the four remaining 1-click installers (Statamic, Craft CMS, Matomo, phpMyAdmin) and four SSL options (ZeroSSL, signing request, DNS-provider validation, HTTP/3).** The installers are written, not guessed — but an installer that has never been RUN is exactly what this codebase's own history warns about, and each needs a real machine; they are the next piece, not a paragraph. |
| 2026-08-10 | **WordPress's own timer, and search-and-replace across the database** (`wp_security_service.cron_schedule`/`check_can_disable_timer`/`build_timer_command`/`check_terms`/`build_search_replace_command`) | Two more of Ploi's WordPress items, and both are ways to break a site quietly rather than loudly. **The timer.** By default WordPress runs its scheduled work during a visitor's page load: on a quiet site scheduled posts publish late or never, and on a busy one every visitor pays for it. The fix is a real system cron plus `DISABLE_WP_CRON`, and the cron half already exists on the site's Scheduled jobs screen — this is the constant that makes it worth having. **The ordering IS the safety, and it runs both ways:** switching the built-in timer off while no real cron is running stops scheduled work COMPLETELY and silently — no posts publish, no backups run, and nobody finds out until they notice something that never happened, which is strictly worse than the default we started from. So the constant is only ever set once a real job is in place, and on the way back the built-in timer is restored BEFORE the job is removed; there is no moment when neither is running. **Search and replace** is the most dangerous thing on the screen — it rewrites the database in bulk and there is no undo — so three rules, none optional. It goes through **wp-cli, never SQL**: WordPress stores PHP-serialized arrays with byte-LENGTH prefixes, so a plain `UPDATE … REPLACE()` leaves every serialized value with a length that no longer matches its content, and WordPress then **silently discards** those options — widgets vanish, theme settings reset, and nothing reports an error. **`guid` is never touched**: it looks like a URL and is not one, it is a permanent identifier, and rewriting it makes every feed reader treat every existing post as brand new — subscribers get the whole archive again. And **nothing runs until a dry run has been seen**, reported per table, because "412,000 rows" and "3 rows" mean very different things and only the customer can tell which is right. Only this install's own tables are touched — `--all-tables` would reach anything else sharing the database, which on shared hosting is somebody else's site. Deliberately does NOT refuse a short search term: the dry-run count is an exact guard where a rule about length is a guess. **One bug found only by running it against a real wp-cli:** the colour flag was written as `--no-ansi`, which is artisan's, and wp-cli refuses an unknown flag outright — so the command failed on every site. The other three flags were checked against `wp help search-replace` in the same pass rather than one at a time. 34 tests. |
| 2026-08-10 | **Telling someone a deploy happened — Ploi's per-site Notifications** (`deploy_notify_service`, migration 061, `DeployNotifications` on the deploy screen) | **A subscription, not a second notification system.** The destination is a channel the customer already made and verified on Settings, and the sending is `channel_service.deliver` — so there is still exactly ONE implementation of "talk to Slack", and a channel fixed in one place is fixed everywhere. Ploi's other half — a pair of raw webhook URLs POSTed per site — is deliberately **not** copied: we already have signed, retried, delivery-logged webhooks with an event catalogue, so a deploy became two more events in that catalogue rather than an unsigned second path nobody could audit. **A failure to notify must never fail the deploy**: the deploy is the important thing and being told about it is a side effect, so `notify` NEVER raises — a deploy that succeeded must not be reported as failed because Slack was down, and a deploy that failed must not lose its own error behind a second one. The outcome is written onto the rule instead and the screen reads it back, the same honesty as a channel that says "Not tested yet" until a message has actually arrived. `wants()` **fails closed at every ambiguity** — switched off, no channel, or a malformed events list all mean do not send, because being silent is recoverable and sending to the wrong place is not. An **empty event set is refused rather than saved**, since a rule that fires on nothing looks configured and does nothing, and the customer believes they are covered. And the message **names the site first**: one that only says "deploy failed" makes somebody open the app to find out which of their sites it was, which is exactly the work it was supposed to save. 21 tests. |
| 2026-08-10 | **Three of Ploi's four remaining SSL options — ZeroSSL, a signing request, and HTTP/3** (`ssl_service.check_authority`, `cert_install_service` CSR half, new `http3_service`) | **(1) ZeroSSL** exists for one narrow reason: Let's Encrypt allows five certificates per domain per week, and an agency adding subdomains to one domain reaches that in an afternoon — after which every attempt fails for days with an error that reads like a configuration problem. It needs External Account Binding, which is a credential, so **it never goes on a command line**: certbot reads it from a mode-600 config file that a `trap` removes however the script ends — the same shape the offsite-backup URL uses. The test that matters records what certbot ACTUALLY received: a stub dumps its own argv and the HMAC key must be absent from it and present only in the file. Reading the script also found a real latent bug — `[ -n "$X" ] && printf …` at the end of a list returns non-zero when X is empty, and under `set -e` that exits the script silently before certbot is ever called; an authority needing no account binding would have died there. **(2) The signing request** is the half that comes before installing a certificate you bought. **The key and the request are made on the SERVER and the key never leaves it** — generating them here would mean the private key passing through our process and our database, which is exactly what the install path avoids by using SFTP. When the certificate comes back, the install form finds the key already waiting, so nobody handles it at all. The subject fields are refused rather than escaped, because `/` and `=` are openssl's own separators inside `-subj` and either would silently add a field to the certificate. **(3) HTTP/3** carries the sharpest edge of the three, and both halves were proven in containers rather than reasoned about. **Most servers cannot do it**: nginx needs `--with-http_v3_module`, which arrived in 1.25, and Ubuntu 24.04 — what our own setup installs — ships 1.24; on a real 24.04 the probe reports `quic=no`, and forcing it anyway makes nginx refuse with `invalid parameter "quic"` while the file stays byte-identical and the site keeps serving. So a server that cannot is told why rather than handed a switch that would take down every site on the machine. And **`reuseport` may appear only once per address:port in the whole configuration** — on real nginx 1.27, a second site claiming it produces `duplicate listen options for 0.0.0.0:443` and nginx will not start, so whether to write it is read off the server rather than assumed, and this site holding it does not count against itself. The third trap is silent rather than loud: without `Alt-Svc` the listener exists, the port is open, and not one visitor ever tries it — confirmed live that the header really reaches the response. UDP, not TCP, in the firewall, for the same reason. 63 tests across the three; **all 19 mutations killed**, including four on the endpoint (writing when the server cannot parse quic, assuming reuseport is free, blocking the OFF path, and allowing a panel server). One survivor was my own test's fault and worth recording: a stub openssl that failed BEFORE writing anything proved nothing about cleanup, so the cleanup could be deleted entirely and the test still passed — the realistic failure writes the key and then fails. **DNS-provider validation is the one still missing, deliberately.** Ploi puts the customer's DNS token on the server via a certbot plugin; ours already holds those credentials, and the offsite-backup precedent says the managed server should never receive them — so the right build is to run the DNS-01 exchange from the backend and install the result through the certificate path that now exists. That is its own piece, not a paragraph. |
| 2026-08-10 | **Phase 0 — a scan that could not look no longer says it found nothing** (new `privilege.py`, `threat_service` escalation + verdict rule, `threat_worker` incident guard, migration 063, Security page) | Found while onboarding a real AWS EC2. **Every cloud image ships with root SSH disabled** — AWS, Google Cloud and Azure all do — so the ordinary way to connect is `ubuntu` or `ec2-user` with passwordless sudo. Our probes assumed they were already root and mostly never asked. Measured on that live server as `ubuntu`: `/usr/local/lsws/conf/vhosts` denied, both site folders denied, `/var/log/nginx` denied, and `[ -x /usr/bin/cyberpanel ]` **silently false** because the user cannot traverse `/usr/local/CyberCP`. A `find` denied everything prints nothing; every checker reads nothing as **pass**. So the malware scan reported **"No threats found"** — not "I could not look." A false all-clear on the most safety-critical feature we ship, and the same shape as the bug `threat_service._t` was already written to avoid. **The fix is one rule, and it is the whole design: bad news may be reported while blind, good news may not.** Finding malware with half the disk unreadable is still finding malware and acting on it is right; finding *nothing* while blind is not a result. So `_summarize` returns `unknown` rather than `clean` when any privileged check was skipped, while a confirmed webshell still reports `compromised`. `privilege.py` holds both halves — a `PRELUDE` that sets `$SA_SUDO` once (`sudo -n`, never prompting, because there is no terminal on this connection), and the rules a caller uses to refuse a comfortable conclusion. **`parse` fails CLOSED**: anything unreadable is `none`, because "we do not know what we had" must never become "we had everything". Probes now escalate, and which ones need it was **checked on the live server rather than assumed** — `/etc/passwd` is 644 and `ld.so.preload` is world-readable, so the accounts probe is honest without root and marking it privileged would report a "could not check" that is not true; `/proc/1/exe` genuinely is denied, so that one escalates. A new `Section` defaults to `needs_root=True` — a forgotten declaration costs a needless "could not check", which is the mistake we can live with. **Checking the callers found a second instance of the same bug:** the worker closed an open incident whenever the verdict was `not in _ALERTING`, and `unknown` is not in that set — so a compromised server whose connection was later changed to a non-root user would have its incident CLOSED as "came back clean". Now it closes only on a real `clean`. **Proven by running it, in a container with a real webshell planted in a mode-700 site folder:** as `root` → compromised, both shells found; as a user with passwordless sudo → **compromised, both shells found** (the escalation works — before this change that user found nothing); as a user with no sudo → **`unknown`, five checks skipped**, where it previously said "No threats found". Migration 063 stores `privilege` and `skipped` so the history keeps the distinction, and old rows are left NULL rather than back-filled with "root" — claiming a past scan had full access is exactly the guess this change exists to stop. The Security page gained a third state, because `unknown` means two different things and saying the wrong one is its own dishonesty: a scan that never ran is "Not scanned", a scan that ran blind is **"Could not check everything"** with the reason and the names of the skipped checks. 25 tests; **all 9 mutations killed**, including restoring the original false-clean and re-opening the worker's incident-closing hole. Two existing eval tests failed correctly on the way — their fake server never printed a privilege line, so the scan read it as blind and skipped everything, which is the new behaviour working. 3,354 backend pass, 163 vitest, build clean. Still to do in Phase 0: `site_service` has the same 0-sudo problem, so an empty Sites list on a non-root connection still reads as "no websites" rather than "could not look". |
| 2026-08-10 | **Phase 0, second half — a site scan that could not look no longer decides your websites are gone** (`site_service` escalation + a required `complete` flag, duplicate-dict-key sweep) | The same rule as the malware scan, where the consequence is worse. `sync` marks a site it did not see `is_present=False`, and the 2 August uptime work then PAUSES that site's monitoring — so one scan on a non-root connection marks every live website "no longer found" and silences its checks. **Measured on the owner's real CyberPanel server, which serves two production sites: as `root` the probe returned 6 lines; as `ubuntu` it returned ZERO.** The probe now escalates and reports what it could read, and after the fix `ubuntu` returns exactly what root returns. **The guard is `sync(..., complete: bool)` with NO default** — forgetting it is a TypeError at the call rather than a silent wrong answer, the same reason `ssh_service._get_client` made its fingerprint keyword-only after three callers quietly skipped host-key verification. When it is False nothing is marked absent, while everything found is still recorded: **you may add what you saw, you may conclude nothing from what you did not.** **Running it found a bug reading could not:** `$SA_SUDO ls -d /usr/local/lsws/conf/vhosts/*/` expands the glob as the UNPRIVILEGED user first, so on a directory it cannot read the pattern matches nothing and `ls` gets the literal string — a live OpenLiteSpeed server reported no sites while sudo was working perfectly, and both real websites were lost from that source. The glob now expands inside the escalated shell. **And a second bug I introduced and the language hid:** the scan endpoint ended up with two `"note"` keys in one dict literal, so Python kept the last and the privilege warning silently vanished — no error, no warning. Fixed by saying both facts in one note, and generalised into a **duplicate-dict-key sweep** over all of `app/`, next to the redefinition sweep added this morning for the same family of silent-winner bug. Seven existing tests failed on the way, every one of them loudly and correctly — four calling `sync` without saying whether the scan was complete, three unpacking three values from a `discover` that now returns four; the fakes in `test_site_settles_after_install` were updated to model the real signature, because a fake that does not stops proving anything about the real caller. 15 tests, **all 7 mutations killed** (including restoring the glob bug and giving `complete` a default). 3,369 backend pass, 163 vitest, build clean. **Phase 0 is complete.** |
| 2026-08-11 | **Assets, Phase 1 — three questions instead of one list; six tiles become three, and a control panel stops being a kind of machine** (new `lib/assetGroups`, `ADD_TILES`/`groupFor`/`ASSET_FILTERS`; plan in [docs/ASSETS-AND-CLOUD-PLAN.md](docs/ASSETS-AND-CLOUD-PLAN.md) Part A) | The old registry answered three different questions with one list of "categories", which is why the list kept growing: every new answer to any of them became another tile AND another group. Split into **what can I add** (a choice, so it must be short), **where does it live** (derived), and **show me only these** (slices across the groups). **Bare Metal and VPS were one thing wearing two hats** — a customer does not care whether the machine is dedicated or virtual, only that it is Linux and they have SSH, and nothing in the product behaved differently; two choices only created doubt at the moment of adding. **The Hosting Panel tile went because it was a dead end**: read off a live CyberPanel on 4 July, its admin API has no website, database or SSL endpoint at all — the reliable surface is the CLI over SSH, which a Server with a panel already gets, so a tile leading anywhere else is a trap. **Windows Server and Windows (RDP) became one tile with one question inside it** — merge the CHOICE, not the types, because RDP has no command channel and that box must still get *Open desktop* and no shell. **The owner's improvement on my draft was removing the hosting GROUP too**, in their own words: *"I know Panel2 is a server but also a CyberPanel."* It makes the rule pure — a group answers *where did this come from*, and "has a panel" is a property of a machine, not a kind of machine — so it is a chip named after the real panel, and the raw `cyberpanel` text left the fact line rather than being said twice. **The group is derived on every read, never stored**, which is the 2 August server-role lesson applied one level up, and it matters more here because two doors wrote `servers.category` and disagreed: a CyberPanel EC2 imported from AWS was filed as a plain VPS while the same machine added by hand was filed as a panel. So the Add form stopped sending a category and Edit lost its Category selector — there is nothing left to choose, and a stored answer could only ever disagree with the real one. **Filters find things wherever they live, which is the one thing a group cannot do**: forty CyberPanel servers would all sit under Servers anyway, so a Panels *group* would just be "most of my servers" and sort nothing. Zero-count filters are **absent, not disabled**, and the groups stay visible while filtering so a filtered view still says where each result lives. Two smaller guards, both written because the obvious version is wrong: a port the customer TYPED survives a transport switch while one we chose is replaced, and a filter whose last match disappears is ignored rather than left applied with its pill gone and nothing to click. Deleted `ServerCard.tsx`, dead since the July list rebuild. 13 tests, **all 7 mutations killed** — reading the stored category again, dropping RDP from Windows, showing a zero-count filter, treating `unknown` as needing attention, restoring the hosting tile, dropping the Windows command-line method, and defining a panel as `connection_type == hosting` each fail exactly their own test. **Verified live on the real account**: `All 5 · Panels 2 · Windows 1` with *Needs attention* correctly absent because all five are online, both CyberPanel servers chipped, the RDP box under Windows servers still offering Desktop and not Terminal, the Windows tile cascading 3389/5985 with its own explanation, a typed port 2222 surviving the switch to Server, and the Dashboard's composition bar now reading the SAME rule (4 + 1) instead of deriving it separately. Clean-room retested after a dev-server restart — the `assetCategories` console errors were stale HMR from the deleted file, and a fresh load is clean. 172 vitest, build clean. |
| 2026-08-11 | **Assets, Phase 2 — the cloud import looks at what it just added, and the thing it was not looking at was host-key verification** (new `server_probe` shared by both doors; `BackgroundTasks` on `/cloud-accounts/{id}/import`) | The plan asked for a small fix: the AWS import wrote a row and never probed, so an imported CyberPanel EC2 became a plain VPS with no OS and no Control-panel section, while the identical machine added by hand became a panel. **Reading the code to fix it found something the plan had not seen.** The manual add also PINS the host key on first connect, and `ssh_service` compares only when there is something to compare against — `if want_fp and fingerprint != want_fp` — so a NULL pin means **no verification at all**. Every other writer of that pin is a button a person has to press ("Test connection", "Trust new key"); **nothing automatic pins a server**, and the metrics worker that connects every five minutes does not. So a cloud-imported machine connected **unverified on every connection until somebody happened to press Test**, and the "Server identity changed" alert that caught a rebuilt box on 3 August could never fire for it. That is the same shape as the bug found that day, one door up: a verification step that is easy to not call. **The fix is the plan's own rule — the same code, not a copy.** `server_probe.probe` is now the one place a new server is looked at, and `record_os` the one place `detect_os`'s answer is written down; there were **three** copies of that second one (manual add, import, and the "Detect system" button), and each had to remember that a control panel changes what the asset IS. Two structural tests keep it that way — no router may pin a fingerprint of its own, and no router may read `detect_os` without the shared recorder — so the next copy fails at the test rather than in production a month later. **The import probes in the background**, because fifty machines is minutes and the request would time out behind any proxy, and it probes **five at a time**: that cap is not about our resources but theirs — fifty simultaneous SSH connections from one address is what a brute-force looks like, and fail2ban is on many of these machines, including by our own hardening playbook. **Mutation testing found two of my own tests were weak**: one asserted `peak <= CONCURRENCY`, which is self-referential (raise the cap to 100 and the bound rises with it, so it could never fail on the thing it exists to prevent) — it now also checks a fixed ceiling that does not move; and the OS test never covered detection failing while CONNECTED, where writing an empty result would blank the provider's `linux` and leave the row knowing less than before we looked. A third was wrong outright: `\.fingerprint\s*=\s*(?!None)` matched `==` and also backtracked to zero spaces so it matched ` None` too, so it flagged comparisons as writes; the right-hand side is checked in Python now. 14 tests, **all 13 mutations killed**. **Proven against a real machine**: a throwaway row shaped exactly as the old import produced it (no pin, `os_type='linux'`, no panel, `category='vps'`) was handed to the real background function and came back pinned `ssh-ed25519 SHA256:F5Ym…`, `panel=cyberpanel`, `ubuntu 22.04 x86_64`, `category=hosting`, online — and `ssh-keyscan` independently confirms that fingerprint is that host's genuine ed25519 key, so the pin matches the server rather than merely matching our own code. Row deleted afterwards; no existing row touched. **A finding worth keeping:** all five online servers hold **bare** pins with no key type, the old format, so every cold connection to them still tries each key type until one matches. Moving `infer_category` to its proper home broke two existing test files at import — loudly and correctly. 3,382 backend pass, 172 vitest, build clean. |
| 2026-08-11 | **Assets, Phase 3 — a cloud account is a place, not a label** (`groupFor` gains the provider branch, `assetZones`, zone headings that ARE the account, a Cloud section in the nav, `?zone=`) | The owner's rule, in their words: *each provider's assets show only under that provider*. An imported machine now lives in its account's zone **and nowhere else** — it is no longer also listed under Servers, and its account beats what kind of machine it is, so an imported Windows EC2 sits under AWS rather than under Windows servers. Nobody thinks *"my VPS group"*; they think *"my AWS"*, and for an agency one zone per client account is already how they hold it. **The zone IS the account**, so Manage and Console moved onto the heading and `CloudAccountRow` was deleted — a separate row for the account was the same thing listed twice, the duplication rule that has now bitten this codebase four times. **A connected account with nothing imported still gets a zone**, because otherwise connecting an account and importing nothing makes the account itself invisible with no way back to it. **The guard that matters is the one against vanishing.** A server whose `cloud_account_id` points at an account the page cannot see would be filed into a zone nothing draws and disappear entirely — indistinguishable from having been deleted — so it falls back to Servers instead. That case is reachable for real: the accounts query can be in flight, or fail, while the servers have already loaded. Pinned by a test that asserts **every asset appears in exactly one zone whatever the accounts say**, which is a stronger property than checking any single placement. Disconnecting an account needs no code at all — the FK is `SET NULL`, so the same three-line rule re-files the asset, which is what deriving it buys. The nav follows the plan's refinement: **one** Cloud item with the accounts inside, not one item per provider (an agency with twenty accounts would otherwise get twenty top-level rows), and **absent entirely until an account exists** — a heading that always says "nothing here" is worse than no heading. Each links to `?zone=`, which narrows the page and composes with the filter pills rather than replacing them; a link to an account that has since been disconnected says so instead of rendering a blank page. 21 tests, **all 6 mutations killed** (dropping the cloud branch, letting the machine type win over its account, removing the orphan guard, hiding an empty account, rendering empty generic groups, and putting the providers last). **Verified live** — the owner has no cloud accounts, so this UI could not be seen at all without throwaway rows: two fake accounts (AWS + Hetzner) and one imported server produced the AWS zone with its EC2 (and **no provider badge on the row**, since the heading already says AWS), the empty Hetzner zone still reachable, the EC2 **absent** from Servers, the sidebar Cloud section, the zone link narrowing to `ceo@astgd.com` with a back link, and a dead zone id reporting honestly; both themes, then all three rows deleted and the account count confirmed back at zero. Clean-room retested after a dev-server restart — the `assetCategories` and `CloudAccountRow` console errors were stale HMR from the deleted files. 180 vitest, build clean. |
| 2026-08-11 | **BUG-018/019/020 FIXED — the deploy skill learns what the code already knew, and the seam is code-vs-prompt** (`app/skills/github-deploy.md`; 4 contract tests in `tests/test_ally_evals.py`); see `docs/ISSUES-FOUND.md` | Found live: the owner asked Ally to deploy `github.com/ASTGD/rcmaa` to `rcmalumni.astgd.com` on Panel2 and it **blocked at 23 of 25 steps**. The good half first — it surveyed the box, backed the live site up (62 MB), cloned to a STAGING folder and stopped honestly without touching production. The bad half is where the steps went. The site's `composer.lock` needs PHP ≥ 8.4.1 while `command -v php` is 8.3.30, so Ally correctly hunted for 8.4, found `/usr/local/lsws/lsphp84/bin/lsphp`, and hit Composer's *"cannot be run safely on non-CLI SAPIs"*. It then spent **ten steps** on that one wall: `sed`-ing `register_argc_argv` in the LIVE web `php.ini` (twice), retrying `-d`/`-c` flag orders five times, adding `ppa:ondrej/php` (no 8.4 for *focal*), and trying to fetch a static PHP. At step 17 it listed that very directory — whose output contains a `php` binary — and never tried it. Verified on the box: `/usr/local/lsws/lsphp84/bin/php -v` → **`PHP 8.4.20 (cli)`**, `PHP_SAPI === "cli"`. **The uncomfortable part is that we already knew.** `laravel_service._prelude` has globbed `/usr/local/lsws/lsphp*/bin/php` since 4 August, proving each candidate with `artisan --version` — the fix for this exact CyberPanel PHP problem. But Ally follows the SKILL, and the skill said only `composer install --no-dev`. **One rule living in code and in prose, with only one half taught** — a seam the "one rule, one place" habit had never covered, because both halves look like they are doing their own job. The skill now resolves PHP from the same list, runs Composer as `$PHP /usr/local/bin/composer`, and carries three pitfalls: `lsphp` is the web SAPI and **no setting can change that**; a **usage banner plus exit 0 means the ARGUMENT was refused**, from any tool (Ally read the banner as "ran and still blocked" and retried five times); and **never edit shared web-server configuration to make a CLI tool run** — that `php.ini` serves all 77 sites on the panel, and it was only restored because the mission happened to keep going. Also told the owner their repo disagrees with itself: `composer.json` says `^8.3` while the lock pins Symfony 8.1.x needing 8.4.1. 4 contract tests, **all 8 mutations killed** — and three of them survived the first attempt because my own assertions were weak: `"sapi" in body` passed on the quoted Composer error rather than the explanation, deleting one sentence of a rule stated twice changed nothing, and the drift test hardcoded the search list so it could only catch a path being REMOVED. That last one now reads the list **out of the service**, so a path added on one side and not the other fails. The final survivor is honestly not a gap — dropping the lsphp path from the service is killed by `test_php_is_looked_for_where_it_actually_lives`, which I confirmed by running the mutation rather than assuming. **Proven end to end on the real server:** the exact command the fixed skill prescribes resolved the whole dependency tree in the staged folder (`--dry-run`, exit 0, Laravel 13.23.0 and 40-odd packages) — one command in place of the ten steps that blocked. |
| 2026-08-11 | **Assets, Phase 4 — AWS Systems Manager as a transport: no key, no open port, nothing to lose** (new `ssm_service`, routed through `connection_manager`) | Everything else in this product dials IN, which needs a reachable address, an inbound rule, and a credential somebody has to hold. That last one is the worst part of an agency's job — fifty clients means fifty `.pem` files, and the key we were handed during the AWS onboarding was **the only copy, in a personal OneDrive, on an account with no MFA**, which is normal rather than unusual. SSM removes all three: the agent dials OUT, so an instance with **no public IP and port 22 shut** is reachable, and access is IAM. It also opens customers we cannot serve at all today, because PCI and HIPAA environments forbid inbound SSH outright. **It fits without touching anything else** — every transport here meets one contract, `execute(server, command) -> (stdout, stderr, exit)`, and `SendCommand` + `GetCommandInvocation` produce exactly that shape, so OS detection, metrics, site discovery, the security and malware scans, logs, databases, cron, daemons, PHP, firewall, playbooks, Ally and missions all work over it unchanged. **No new columns:** an SSM asset has no login of its own, so `cloud_account_id` names the AWS key and `cloud_instance_id` names the target — both already there since migration 027. That has a consequence worth stating rather than discovering: **disconnecting the AWS account does not merely lose a label, it removes the only way to reach the machine** (the FK is SET NULL), so that case says so in those words. **The three ways SSM fails are all quiet, which is why they are the tests.** An unregistered instance answers *nothing* rather than refusing, so `is_managed` fails closed and the message names the fix (agent, `AmazonSSMManagedInstanceCore`, egress — and that **nothing needs opening inward**). Output is cut at **24,000 characters with no flag saying so**, and length is the only signal there is — a malware scan cut short looks exactly like one that found less, which is the same false all-clear Part D was written to stop — so truncation is reported every time, into **stderr** rather than stdout, because appending to stdout would corrupt the sentinel-delimited bundles our probes parse and turn a reporting problem into a parsing one. And a command that never ran has no `ResponseCode`; defaulting that to 0 would report a failure as a success. Two more that decide whether it works at all: the **region comes from the asset**, not the account, because an account left on "all regions" imports from several and guessing gives `InvalidInstanceId` for an instance that exists perfectly well elsewhere; and Windows takes a different SSM document, where sending the wrong one runs nothing. `execute_stream` **says there is no stream** before yielding the result at the end — the apt-lock lesson, that a progress box sitting empty reads as a hang. 16 tests, **all 12 mutations killed**, and the tests were tightened first: they patched module globals without restoring, which hands the next test a stub and calls it a pass, so they use `monkeypatch` and one test asserts the module was left as it was found. **Proven end to end through the real path** — real DB rows, real AES-encrypted account credential, real `connection_manager`, with only AWS itself substituted: `test_connection` ok with a correctly EMPTY fingerprint (identity here is IAM, not a host key), `execute` polling Pending→InProgress→Success to a clean 3-tuple, the asset's `region:eu-west-2` tag beating the account's `us-east-1`, the right document and instance, the command passed through verbatim, **boto3 handed the ACCOUNT's key** and **no key anywhere in what reached the instance**. Throwaway rows removed. **Honest gaps:** no SSM-managed instance was available, so the AWS calls are proven against a stand-in rather than the real API — the same caveat as the hosting adapters and cloud lifecycle — and every item in the plan's *How it gets validated* (a private-subnet instance, port 22 closed, an instance with no role, >24 KB output, cross-account) needs the owner's AWS account. There is also **no door yet**: nothing creates an `ssm` asset, so the transport is complete and unreachable until the import learns to offer it (4b). Files, the terminal and cross-account roles are Phases 5–7 by design. 3,402 backend pass. |
| 2026-08-11 | **Assets, Phase 4b — the door to Systems Manager, and the downgrade it must not quietly hand you** (`cloud_service.transport_for`/`credential_needed`/`_ssm_managed`, `lib/cloudImport`, import + modal) | Phase 4 built the transport and left it unreachable — nothing created an `ssm` asset. Discovery now asks SSM which instances are actually answering, and the import can file one. **The decision that carries the feature is that Systems Manager is the FALLBACK, not the default.** It has no file transfer and no interactive terminal yet, so choosing it for a machine with a perfectly good address would hand the customer a server with no File Manager, no `.env` editor, no certificate install and no terminal — a downgrade they never asked for and would have no way to explain. So an address wins unless they tick the box, and the box states the trade rather than selling the feature. What it unlocks instead is the case SSH **cannot do at all**: an instance with no public and no reachable private address used to be counted as "no reachable IP" and dropped, and if the agent dials out there is nothing to reach. **Nothing is demanded that is not needed** — an all-SSM batch asks for no username and no key, because asking would be asking for the exact artefact SSM exists to remove, while one SSH instance in the batch brings it straight back. That rule lives in TWO places by necessity (the form must agree with the endpoint or the button submits a 422 nobody can diagnose), so the browser half is a named module tested against the same cases as the Python half rather than an expression inline in a component. **Four things had to be got right or the transport would not work at all**, and each is a test: the region travels with the asset (`region:<x>` in its tags) because an account left on "all regions" imports from several and AWS answers `InvalidInstanceId` for an instance that exists perfectly well elsewhere — Phase 4 reads that tag, so the two halves are asserted together; an SSM asset stores `encrypt("")` rather than the batch SSH password, because a secret kept on a row that can never use it is one more thing that can leak; it keeps a recognisable host (private IP, else the instance id) because a blank row is one nobody can identify; and its port is **0**, since writing 22 would be a number that means nothing and reads on the asset page as something a firewall might be blocking. The SSM check is **best-effort**: most keys have no `ssm:DescribeInstanceInformation`, and refusing to list anything because of a permission the customer does not need yet is worse than not offering the option — and only an instance actually answering (`PingStatus == "Online"`) counts, since a registered-but-silent agent would file the asset as reachable and let every later probe return nothing. 17 backend + 11 frontend tests, **all 17 mutations killed** across both halves. **Honest gap, unchanged from Phase 4:** without an AWS account carrying SSM-managed instances the screen itself cannot be driven, so what is proven is the rule and the wiring, not the pixels — the same caveat as the hosting adapters. Connecting a real account makes every item in the plan's *How it gets validated* testable in minutes. 3,419 backend pass, 191 vitest, build clean. |
| 2026-08-11 | **Assets, Phase 5 — connecting a CLIENT's AWS account without holding a credential in it** (new `aws_identity`, `GET /cloud-accounts/aws/role-setup`, role-shaped cloud credentials) | The piece that makes this sellable to an agency. **AWS partners are not given an IAM user in a client's account** — they are given a role they assume, with an external ID. Handing over an access key is what a competent client's security review refuses, and it is also what we least want to hold: a key in fifty client accounts is fifty secrets that can leak, rotate or outlive the engagement. A role is granted and revoked by the client, in their own console, and every command appears in *their* CloudTrail. **The external ID is the entire security of it, and the danger is subtle.** A role that trusts our AWS account is otherwise assumable by ANYONE who can name its ARN — including another of our own customers — because AWS only checks that the *caller* is us. That is the confused-deputy problem, and the only thing standing in front of it is a secret we choose. So a value arriving in the request is **discarded, not merged**: `{**theirs, **ours}` looks safe and leaves their value in place for any key we do not overwrite, which is the version of this bug that survives review. **It is derived, not stored** — an HMAC of the account id under the deployment secret — because it has to survive between two moments (handed over so the client can write their trust policy, used later when the role is connected) and a random value would need somewhere to live in between: a table, or a cache whose expiry is wrong either way, since a customer waiting on their client's IAM approval may take a week. **It is per CUSTOMER rather than per role, and that is a decision rather than a shortcut** — the role ARN does not exist yet at the moment we must hand the value over, and per customer is exactly enough for what it defends: another of our customers naming a role that is not theirs gets a value that does not match, so AWS refuses. The plan said "unique per connection"; this says what was actually built and why, rather than claiming the stronger thing. **Assumed credentials are never stored** — only the ARN and the external ID are, and the keys come from `AssumeRole` per use, cached in memory until five minutes before they expire (a mission running twenty commands should not make twenty extra STS calls, and a credential that dies mid-command turns a working deploy into an AccessDenied halfway through). The **trust policy is produced, not described**, because one typed from a description is one with a typo in it and a typo'd external ID fails as an AccessDenied that reads like a permissions problem — which is why the refusal message names all three things it can actually be, and owns the one that is ours. **Absent, not broken:** with no AWS identity configured the role option is not offered at all, since collecting an ARN we could never assume wastes the customer's time and teaches them the feature does not work. And both AWS callers now resolve a session in **one place** — `cloud_service` discovers, `ssm_service` runs commands, and a second copy is how one of them ends up not knowing roles exist, which is the same code-vs-prompt seam that cost ten steps on a live deploy this morning. 16 tests, **all 12 mutations killed** — one survivor first, honestly a weak mutation rather than a weak test: it replaced only the first line of a multi-line refusal, so the three explanations were still there; gutting the whole message kills it. **Honest gap: proving this live needs a SECOND AWS account** to create a role in, so what is proven is the derivation, the discard, the never-stored property and the single session builder — not a real `AssumeRole`. 3,435 backend pass. |

Migration head `063`, seven containers healthy, and the deploy proven by a probe that changed: `/api/cloud-accounts/aws/role-setup` went **404 → 401** while `/api/servers` stayed 401 as the control — a 404 everywhere would have proved nothing. A live authenticated MCP call then returned real fleet health for all nine servers, so auth, the database and the new code all work together. **A process note worth keeping:** I first told the owner the deploy was theirs to run, having concluded from a single bare `ssh root@…` that there was no access. The harness now shadows `sudo` too, verified by checking the probe takes the escalation path (`privilege|sudo`) AND still parses the site through it. | 2026-08-11 | **Staging P5 — the four things a copy must not inherit** (new `staging_rules`, guards on cron/daemons/auto-deploy/escalation, `site_service.by_domain`) | The plan orders this **before** either promote path, and checking the code showed why that was right: **nothing in the codebase read `sites.environment` at all.** A staging copy was an ordinary site row in every respect — which is the design, and is what makes Files, Logs, Database and PHP work on it the day it exists — but it meant a copy of a live site could be given a cron job, a queue worker, push-to-deploy and a pager, by inheritance, with nobody deciding. Each of the four is an incident rather than a feature: a copied **WordPress** running the live schedule sends the same customer email **twice**; a staging **queue worker** pointed at the live queue *consumes* jobs, so the live site's work silently stops happening with no error anywhere; **push-to-deploy** would make one push land on the copy AND the live site, which is the opposite of what staging is for; and a copy that **pages at 3am** is how a team learns to ignore the pager for the live site too. **Staging is decided by the column, never the domain** — somebody who named a site `staging.shop.com` by hand is not a copy of anything, and deciding by name would both refuse a real production site and miss a genuine copy called something else. **On the monitor question the plan said read the wiring rather than guess, so I did:** an uptime failure reaches `incident_service.raise_for` through `_escalate_down`, and the answer is a monitor that never escalates rather than no monitor — with none the Sites row reads *"Not checked"*, and a row that says nothing is worse than one that says down. The customer should be able to SEE the copy is broken without being woken about it. One rule module, because the cron page, the daemons page, the deploy screen and the uptime worker all have to agree and four copies of "is this staging?" is four chances for one to say no; and each refusal explains what it protects, since a refusal that only says no teaches the customer the product is broken. 17 tests, **all 10 mutations killed** — including removing each of the four guards individually. | 2026-08-11 | **Staging P3 + P4 — putting a copy live, by commit or by file** (new `promote_service`, `deploy_service.build_plan(commit=…)`) | **P3 is the existing deploy plus one new idea.** Build in a folder nobody serves, atomic `mv -T`, shared paths preserved, a failed build never reaching the live site, rollback after — all of it comes free, because it is the same path proven on a real server on 28 July. The new idea is the **pin**: `build_plan` deploys a BRANCH, and promotion has to deploy a COMMIT, or "put staging live" quietly means "deploy whatever is on the branch now" — and between opening the page and pressing the button, a teammate can push. **The plan's assumption about where to find that commit was wrong:** `DeployTarget.current_release` sounds like a commit and holds a release-folder timestamp, so the commit is read off the server through `current` — the symlink a visitor's request actually follows, which differs from the newest folder the moment somebody has rolled back. The clone is `--depth 1`, so the pin deepens only when the commit is not already there, and **the checkout is verified**: one that fails is loud, but one that silently lands elsewhere would put unreviewed code live while every step reported success. Proven against a **real git repository** rather than a string — two commits, check out the older one and confirm HEAD moved, then ask for a commit that does not exist and confirm it stops. Repositories are compared by what they point at, so `git@github.com:acme/shop.git` and the https URL are not refused as different; a genuine mismatch names **both** URLs, because "repositories do not match" leaves somebody comparing them by eye. **P4 is the only operation in the product that can destroy a working website**, so every rule is a refusal rather than a warning — a warning is a thing people click through — and every exclusion is proven by RUNNING the copy against a real tree and asserting the file on the destination is unchanged, since `--exclude=.env` appearing in a command is true whether or not rsync honours it. `.env` and `wp-config.php` never travel (the live site would start reading the staging database — the worst outcome this feature can produce); uploads never travel (staging's are a stale snapshot, and copying them **deletes every file a customer has uploaded since**); the database is never touched at all. Backup first, and a failure there stops everything — proven by stubbing `tar` to fail and confirming the live file is untouched. **Running it found a real bug that reading could not:** `rsync -a` skips any file whose size AND mtime match, so a one-line edit made on staging in the same second, to the same length, was **silently not copied** — a promotion where one file quietly did not arrive is the worst failure this can have, since everything reports success and the site is subtly wrong. `--checksum` now, which is slower and correct, and a promotion is a rare deliberate act where that is the right trade. Replacing a DIRECTORY takes two renames — there is no portable atomic swap for directories the way `mv -T` gives one for a symlink — so that is stated rather than claimed, and the old directory is kept under a name the message gives so a failed second rename is put back. 26 tests, **all 18 mutations killed** — three survived first and all three were weak tests of mine: a mutation that gutted only the first line of a two-line verification, no test at all that a PRODUCTION site is refused, and an anchor that had moved. 3,451 backend pass. |
| 2026-08-12 | **The door to promote — and the access hole that only exists once there is a caller** (`GET`/`POST /api/sites/{id}/promote`, new `promote_runner`, `promote_service.check_layouts_match`, `PromoteToLive` card) | P3 and P4 shipped their logic on 11 August with **no endpoint, no router and no UI** — complete, proven work that nobody could reach, which is exactly what Phase 4 (SSM) had done a day earlier. I had also told the owner "both promote paths ship", which overstated it. **The most dangerous input this endpoint could accept is a commit**, so it does not: `PromoteIn` carries only the method and the typed domain, and the commit is read off the server at the moment of pressing. A commit in the request body would let anyone who can promote deploy ANY revision to a live website, including one nobody reviewed — and an unreadable one refuses rather than falling through, because `build_plan` without a commit deploys the branch tip, which is the one thing the pin exists to prevent. **The confirmation has to be the customer's words**: the GET passes the live domain deliberately (it is asking *would this be offered*), the POST passes what was typed, and mixing those up would make the confirmation confirm itself. **A new guard the service did not have:** both sites must keep their application in the same place — a Laravel site keeps it above the folder it serves, so copying one of those onto a site laid out the other way publishes `.env`, the database password in a text file at a guessable address; refused in both directions, for the same reason `clone_service.destination_target` refuses it. **Reading my own code found a hole that unit tests would not have:** the live site was loaded by id with no ownership check, and `deploy_runner.start_deploy` performs no access check of its own — the deployments router does it before calling — so the Git path could have deployed onto a target the caller has no rights to. Not reachable today, since only the staging endpoint writes `parent_site_id`; closed anyway, because of where it LEADS. And three ordering assertions broke the moment I wrote a COMMENT mentioning `start_deploy`, because `body.index()` matched the sentence about the call rather than the call: the fourth time this codebase has been bitten by a too-loose anchor (`pgrep`, `sshd -t`, an import line), so the tests now read executable lines only. 22 tests, **all 12 mutations killed**. **Proven against the real app** — real routing, real dependencies, real auth, real database, throwaway rows removed after: the copy's page offers both paths with the Git one honestly unavailable; a wrong or empty domain is refused with the exact wording; no method chosen is refused rather than guessed; a site that is not a copy is refused; and the RIGHT domain in the wrong case PASSED the confirmation and was then refused **by the server** ('this site's folder is not on the server'), which is the ordering working — nothing is copied before the server is asked. Verified in the browser in both themes, and the card is **absent** on an ordinary site rather than present-and-unavailable. **A process note:** the full suite reported two phantom failures because I ran the mutation script, which rewrites source files, while that suite was still in flight — the same class of mistake as several pytest processes against one dev Postgres. |
| 2026-08-12 | **BUG-021 FIXED — a staging copy records its own database, so removing the copy takes it with it** (`staging_service.build_record_db`/`creds_path`/`build_forget_db_record`, `staging_runner._undo`) | Found by running the whole promote flow on a real server rather than by reading it: both test sites were removed with *drop database* ticked, the LIVE site's database was dropped and the copy's survived. **The removal was not at fault** — it said *"No database was recorded for this site, so none was removed"* rather than guessing a name, which is exactly right: guessing from the domain would drop a database belonging to another site. Nothing had written the record. **The bug is a seam between two places** — one place makes a database, a DIFFERENT script drops it, and nothing checked they agreed. `site-remove` drops the database named in `/root/<domain>_db.txt`; every installer writes that file; the staging path made a database and never wrote it. So the fix is at the producer and **removal is unchanged**. My first guess was wrong in a way worth recording: I assumed the record lived in the site's install VARIABLES, and reading the consumer showed the removal passes only DOMAIN and DROP_DB to a script that reads a FILE — the real fix was smaller than the guess, and reading the consumer first is what found it. Three properties: the record is written **last**, past the guard that tears down a copy which could not be repointed, so it can only exist for a copy that really has its own database; `_undo` removes it alongside the database it names, since a file pointing at a dropped database would have the remover fail about something that was never there; and a copy with NO database writes nothing, because a file claiming one would have the remover try to drop a database called nothing. One later command that fetched a single web page produced **~45 records, about 12 KB of markup**. The old cleaner only dropped a `#< CLIXML` preamble line that pywinrm had already stripped, so the whole body went through. **Not cosmetic:** `execute_stream` yields std_err into the output stream and the playbook runner prints it, so every Windows playbook run would have shown a wall of XML; several callers read a non-empty std_err as failure; and MCP raised its `truncated` flag on results that were complete. One claim I made and then checked was wrong — stdout is capped BEFORE std_err, so the real output was never crowded out. Now only `Error` and `Warning` records survive, `_x000D_` escapes and XML entities are decoded, records split across a long message are joined into a sentence, and a std_err holding nothing else becomes empty — the honest answer, because nothing was wrong. Anything that is not CLIXML is passed through untouched, so a transport failure (`WinRM error: …`) cannot be swallowed by a parser built for a different shape. **The test fixture is the exact payload the live box sent, kept verbatim** — an invented sample would only prove the parser handles what I expected, which is how this survived in the first place. 14 tests, **all 7 mutations killed**. 3,482 backend pass. |
| 2026-08-12 | **BUG-023 FIXED — a Windows security audit that produced nothing readable and reported success** (`win-audit`, `win-openssh`, `win-iis`; contract test over every PowerShell playbook) | The first Windows playbook ever run on a real machine. It exited 0 in 13 seconds and its report had the OS table printed **underneath the *Local Users* heading**, with every later section a run of blank padded rows — the worst shape a bug can have, since nothing reports a problem and the output is worthless. **Two PowerShell behaviours, neither visible without a real box:** `Write-Host` writes to the information stream while `Get-*` sends OBJECTS down the success pipeline, and remoting renders the two separately and merges them at the end, so a heading and the data it labels arrive apart; and objects of DIFFERENT types in one pipeline are all formatted to the FIRST object's shape, so everything after it renders blank. Fixed by converting each block to text where it is produced (`| Out-String`), emitting headings as plain strings so everything travels in ONE stream, saying *(nothing to report)* rather than leaving a gap that reads as failure, and `-ErrorAction SilentlyContinue` per cmdlet so one missing on a trimmed edition of Windows costs one section rather than the whole report. **The rewrite was proven on the live box before being written into the playbook** — it now returns the OS, four accounts with last-logon, the Administrators group, 13 listening ports and all three firewall profiles. The same one-line trap in `win-openssh` and `win-iis` fixed too, and the guard is a contract over EVERY PowerShell playbook rather than this one, because the trap is one careless line away in any of them — **verified by restoring the original broken script and watching the tests fail.** Same root cause as BUG-022 and found in the same hour: the Windows playbooks were authored and syntax-checked and never run. Checking why found a control being walked around rather than a missing feature: **`read_file` MASKS secrets over MCP** — deliberately, since there is no client-side redaction out there — so an AI needing an application's credentials cannot get them through the proper tool, and its workaround is a full shell with none of ServerAlly's higher protections and no rollback. A 900 KB upload returned **HTTP 500 — and the file was on the server, complete**: `upload_file` was declared `-> dict[str, str]` and returns `"size": len(data)`, an int, so FastAPI rejected **our own response** — *after* the SFTP write. The second fault only appears above 1 MB — `frontend/nginx.conf` never set `client_max_body_size`, so nginx's **1 MB default** answered with its own raw HTML 413 and the file never arrived.
