The tests drive the REAL app, because the fault lived in FastAPI's response validation and a direct call to the handler returns the dict happily. **Mutation testing corrected the fix twice:** two size checks were each individually removable because the other caught it — no test could tell which had gone — so they became ONE named rule used at two moments (refuse before reading, then refuse what actually arrived) with a test for each purpose; and `limit=MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES` written as a default argument is bound at import and can never be changed afterwards, including by a test, so it is read at call time now. 12 tests, **all 7 mutations killed** — including restoring `dict[str, str]` and removing the nginx line, the two shipped states. 3,545 backend pass. |
| 2026-08-18 | **ChatGPT can connect — a client's own declared scope was never the gate** (`oauth_provider.requestable_scope` applied in `get_client`; `default_scopes = ALL_SCOPES`) | Owner reported the MCP connector failing at OAuth from ChatGPT. **The production access log gave the exact answer in one read, which no amount of code-reading would have:** our own `/authorize` answered `error=invalid_scope — Client was not registered with scope mcp:write`. The stored clients told the rest — `Claude scope = "mcp:read mcp:write mcp:admin"`, `ChatGPT scope = "mcp:read"`. **Claude declares its scopes when it registers; ChatGPT does not.** A client that omits `scope` at Dynamic Client Registration is stamped with the server's default (`register.py:62`), and every scope it later asks for is checked against that stamp (`shared/auth.py:99`). ChatGPT then reads our metadata and asks for the three scopes we publish in `scopes_supported` — so **we refused a client for asking for exactly what we advertised**, our own metadata contradicting itself. **What makes widening it safe is that the stamp was never a control:** what a connection is GRANTED comes only from the access level the person picks on the consent page (`scopes_for_access_level`), which overrides the client's request entirely — so the declaration could only ever refuse a legitimate connection, never restrain one. `default_scopes` looked like "a new connection is read-only by default", which is a good rule enforced somewhere else; what it actually set was a ceiling taken from a field the client is free to omit. Applied **on READ**, so it is a live property rather than a value frozen at registration — the same lesson as the `_latest` staleness fix, that a guard belongs at the read and not at one call site. 14 tests, **all 6 mutations killed**, including restoring the shipped bug and letting consent grant what the client asked for instead of what the person chose. **Proven live against production after deploying:** a registration in ChatGPT's exact shape (no `scope` field) is now stamped with all three, and — the half that matters — **the already-broken client ChatGPT is using (`3dac4e36…`) reached the consent page instead of `invalid_scope`**, so the owner does not have to remove and re-add anything. The tokens table also shows ChatGPT connected successfully on 13 and 16 August with full power; today's failure was the Windows desktop app, which is the client that sends an explicit scope. Fourth entry in [[mcp-first-connect-deploy-gotchas]] — every one of the four was found by reading what the client actually requested, not our code. 3,601 backend collected, suite green. |
| 2026-08-18 | **BUG-025 FIXED — two commands at once to a Windows server broke both, and everything after** (`winrm_service._session_for` + a per-server `threading.Lock`; `_get_session` deleted) | The per-server lock had been agreed as a plan and never built, and Windows is newly in real use for the .NET verifier engine, where an AI client runs several commands. **Measured against the real box through the real service before touching anything: 8 concurrent commands, ZERO succeeded** — the first returned `SpnegoError (6): invalid MIC` and the other seven `Bad HTTP response ... Code 500`, the corrupted session cascading. Not intermittent; total failure under any concurrency, persisting until the session is rebuilt. **No instance was ever stored** — it lived in memory and container logs — so it had to be demonstrated rather than recalled, which is the stronger evidence anyway: the stub reported **7 concurrent entries out of 8 commands** before the fix. **The cause is what a `winrm.Session` is:** ONE `requests.Session` whose `auth` is a single `HttpNtlmAuth` — a stateful conversation with its own sequence numbers and message signing — and one `run_ps` is FIVE HTTP calls over it (open_shell → run_command → get_command_output → cleanup_command → close_shell). Cached per server in a plain dict and handed to a 20-thread pool, two commands interleaved two five-call sequences through one signing context, and NTLM's answer to a signed message out of order is the invalid MIC. **Sharing was safe for SSH and that is not luck** — a paramiko client multiplexes independent channels by design — so this needed its own answer rather than a copy of that one. `_session_for` now hands out the session and its lock **together** and `_get_session` is gone, so there is no unguarded way to reach one — the same shape as `ssh_service._get_client` making its fingerprint keyword-only after three callers quietly skipped verification, and an AST sweep fails if any new function touches the cache. **A `threading.Lock`, not an `asyncio.Lock`, and checking rather than assuming is what decided it:** `playbook_tasks` calls `asyncio.run()` **per Celery task**, so a module-level asyncio lock would bind to the first task's loop and raise on every task after it — breaking exactly the Windows playbook path this protects; pinned by a test across three separate loops and by one that READS `playbook_tasks` so the reason stops being an assumption. Per server, never global (a timing test proves two servers still overlap — one lock for the whole service would let a slow box hold up every other). Locks are **never removed**: dropping one while a command still holds it would let the next caller build a fresh lock and run into the same race. **Not retried** — a command that failed on the way back has still run on the server, and quietly running it twice is worse than reporting the failure. 11 tests, **all 5 mutations killed**. **Verified live after deploying: 8/8, then 20/20 at full pool size**, with an ordinary command straight after returning `Microsoft Windows Server 2022 Standard` and empty stderr. Third bug from one gap — Phase 2B was tested entirely against mocked `pywinrm`, and a mock has no NTLM state to corrupt (see BUG-022, BUG-023). |
| 2026-08-18 | **Windows onboarding — the form hands over the one command it cannot run for you** (new `windows_setup_service`, `GET /api/servers/windows-setup`, `WindowsSetupHelp`, `SERVERALLY_EGRESS_IP`) | Adding a Linux server needs nothing done to the server; adding a Windows one does, and the Add Asset form **said nothing about it** — it filled in port 5985 and hoped. When it failed the customer got `HTTPConnectionPool(...): Max retries exceeded ... ConnectTimeoutError`, which names no cause and suggests no action. The owner had asked twice whether the shell command could be avoided: **for a plain Windows VPS it cannot** — remote management cannot be switched on remotely when nothing is on yet, which is the shape of the problem rather than a gap (**AWS is the exception: an SSM-managed instance needs no WinRM, no open port and nothing run on the box**). What *can* be removed is the hunting. **Every fact the command depends on was READ off the real Windows Server 2022 rather than recalled, and two readings changed it:** the box's network profile is **Public** (so plain `Enable-PSRemoting` refuses — `-SkipNetworkProfileCheck` is not optional on a cloud VM), and the rule `Enable-PSRemoting` creates for the Public profile is scoped to **LocalSubnet** — so enabling remoting **does not let us in**, and the explicit firewall rule is genuinely required rather than belt-and-braces. `LocalAccountTokenFilterPolicy` was **unset** on that box and everything worked, because the account is the built-in Administrator — so it is a note shown when relevant, not a registry change weakening a protection for everyone. **The address is STATED, never detected:** detection can be wrong behind a NAT or a second egress, and a wrong address here is worse than none — nothing works and the obvious next move is to open the firewall to everyone; when we do not know it the command carries a placeholder and says so, and it can **never** generate `-RemoteAddress Any`. Failures are now explained from a table built on the strings a **live box actually produced** (closed port, unroutable host, wrong password, missing account, the invalid-MIC handshake, WinRM's own 500), with an unrecognised failure passed through unchanged rather than labelled with a guess. The route is registered before `/{server_id}` — a literal path declared after a path parameter of the same depth is swallowed by it, which is how `/resize` and `/destroy` silently stopped existing in the cloud lifecycle work. 30 tests, **all 9 mutations killed**; one first survived and taught something — a firewall rule **commented out** still passed, because the test asked whether text appeared rather than whether it would RUN, so the tests now read the generated PowerShell the way PowerShell would (the comment-anchor trap, inverted). A second bug was caught only by reading the LIVE output: over-escaping printed `DOMAIN\\\\user` into a message a customer is meant to copy. **Verified live on production** (endpoint returns the scoped command with our real address, 422 on a non-WinRM port, 401 unauthenticated; and the three failure explanations against the real box) and **in the browser end to end** on a throwaway account — both branches (scoped, and the honest 'we do not know' with its amber warning), the conditional note, port 5985, absent for Remote Desktop and for Linux, both themes, zero console errors. |
| 2026-08-18 | **BUG-015 FIXED — ServerAlly refuses a command that would cut its own way in** (`safety_service.lockout_risk` + `Access`/`access_for`, `harden-server.md` Stage 0, the server profile states how we connect) | The one class of damage this product can do to itself, and it had already happened: a `harden-server` mission applied `PermitRootLogin prohibit-password` to a LIVE server ServerAlly reaches as root with a password. Because the step used `reload` rather than `restart` **the session kept working, the step reported SUCCESS**, and the lockout would only have surfaced on the next connection — with no recovery from inside the product. **The reasoning is the part worth keeping:** the model correctly observed that no root SSH key existed and treated that as evidence the change was harmless; the opposite is true — *no key is set up* is exactly what makes it fatal. Its own verification then read `PasswordAuthentication` (which governs normal users) and reported all was well, concealing the damage it had done to root. **Three layers, because a prompt is a request and not a guarantee (BUG-001's lesson):** the code **refuses** it at the one choke point every AI-planned command already passes through — refusing rather than warning, the same call `firewall_service.lockout_risk` makes, because a warning is something a customer clicks through once; the **skill** stops proposing it (nothing in that stage is "safe always" any more, and a new Stage 0 establishes how Ally got in before it touches sshd); and the **prompt** states the facts, without which the model cannot reason about the question at all. The **stored** credential is the right authority, because that is what ServerAlly reconnects with. `PermitRootLogin yes` is deliberately not refused — it is the recovery. **A trap found while building it:** the first version skipped anything `is_read_only_command` called read-only — but despite its docstring that classifier is a DENY-list, so it calls `ssh_set PermitRootLogin prohibit-password` **read-only** and would have waved the incident command straight through; replaced with an explicit allow-list of ways to LOOK, which also catches a read chained to a change, and pinned by a test that asserts the classifier's blind spot still exists and that the guard no longer shares it. 33 tests, **all 12 mutations killed**, plus a structural test that parses every caller and fails if one stops supplying the facts — the `ssh_service._get_client` lesson, where an optional argument left host-key verification off at three call sites. **Verified live against real stored credentials: 12 of 13 Linux servers are reached as root with a password and would have been locked out by that exact command — every one is now refused**, while the one server reached as a non-root user is correctly not blocked and ordinary work is untouched on all 13. The bug log's Open section now holds **zero** genuinely open entries. |
| 2026-08-19 | **The flagship flow, driven for real: WordPress could not be created at all, and the second site of a type always failed** (`site_service._derived_database` in `install_variables`; `site_database_naming.suggest_user` capped) | The owner redirected the work — stop adding, verify and harden what exists, and finish the Sites section first, because that is the product: a raw server is added, ServerAlly sets it up, the customer puts a site on it. Driving that through the real API against a real server found **two bugs in the ordinary path**, neither visible by reading: **`{"domain":…,"site_type":"wordpress"}` → 422 "This installer still needs DB_PASS"** — WordPress, the most common site type there is, could not be created at all; and **the second Laravel site → "a database called 'laravel' already exists"**, because the installers declare FIXED defaults (`wordpress`/`wpuser`, `laravel`/`laravel`) and nothing derived anything per site. Two sites on one server is the ordinary case for an agency. PHP and static were fine — they ask for no database. **Both live in the seam between an installer script and the layer that fills its variables, and each half is correct alone:** the WordPress script generates a password when given none, while the substitution guard — rightly — refuses to pass an empty REQUIRED value, so it never got that far. Fixed by deriving the database name, account and password from the domain in `install_variables`, using **`site_database_naming`, the module the per-site database screen already uses**, so "what is this site's database called" has ONE answer rather than two that drift — and one database plus one account per site is that feature's containment rule. Precedence is the meaning and is pinned: playbook defaults weakest, then what we derive for THIS site, then the customer's own answer, and the domain is nobody's to override. **A latent bug in the naming module fell out too, found by generating names rather than reading them:** `suggest_user` built from the 48-character DATABASE limit while MySQL caps an ACCOUNT at 32 and refuses a longer one outright — by which point the database already exists, leaving half a site. 20 tests, **all 8 mutations killed**, including inverting each precedence rule and letting a backslash back into the generated password. **Proven live on TestServer through the real API:** before, wordpress 422 and the second laravel failed; after deploying, both returned 201 and reached `live` — Laravel served `<title>Laravel</title>`, WordPress served and redirected to its own installer (which is that script's own definition of success: PHP ran and wp-config was read), and each site's config pointed at its **own** database (`wpfix_example_com`, `lvfix_example_com`) with its own account. All five test sites were then removed through the real API — folders, vhosts and databases gone — and the three pre-existing sites verified untouched and still serving. |
| 2026-08-20 | **The raw-server flow, proven end to end on a rebuilt machine — and the three fixes held** (re-run of `add → set up → sites` on a fresh Ubuntu 22.04) | The owner reinstalled TestServerNew so the previous day's three fixes could be proven on hardware rather than argued about. **Every one held.** (1) The rebuilt box read as **fresh**: `already_set_up: false` and yesterday's finished run correctly ignored, because it completed before `identity_changed_at` — the 3 August staleness rule, working on a real replacement rather than a simulated one. (2) Setup reached **14/14 with nothing skipped**, and the summary lost its "1 optional step was skipped" line: the auto-updates step that previously did its job in 3 seconds and was then killed by the idle watchdog after 5 minutes of silence now completes and reports honestly. (3) **PHP 8.3.33 on Ubuntu 22.04** — `laravel_ok=YES`, `php8.3-fpm` running, and the PHP archive added ONLY because 22.04's own PHP is end-of-life; a 24.04 box still gets its own 8.3 and no third-party source. (4) The **self-lockout guard held again**: after "Securing the login", `permitrootlogin yes` and `passwordauthentication yes` — the account ServerAlly actually uses was left alone. Then the whole Sites surface: **six sites created from a domain and a type alone** — static, PHP, WordPress ×2, Laravel ×2 — all live, all serving (static/PHP a real page, Laravel `<title>Laravel</title>`, WordPress its own installer). **The second WordPress and the second Laravel, which failed the day before on a shared database name, both installed**: four databases and four accounts, one per site, each config pointing at its own (`t_wp_example_com`, `t_wp2_example_com`, `t_lv_example_com`, `t_lv2_example_com`). Laravel's document root is `public/` and `/.env` over HTTP returns **403**, so the code and its credentials are not downloadable. Removal proven too: one site removed took its folder and vhost with it, the other five untouched and still serving, and a wrong confirmation refused with 422. **The flagship flow — a raw server added, set up by ServerAlly, and websites put on it — now works end to end for every site type we offer.** |

## 🚀 How to Run Locally

> On this machine, local dev runs on **backend :8888 / frontend :5190** (8000/8080/5173 are used by other local projects) — see **OPS.md** for the exact start/stop commands. The generic commands below use the documented defaults.

```bash
# First time
cd servermind
cp .env.example .env
# Edit .env — add ANTHROPIC_API_KEY minimum

# Start infrastructure
docker compose up -d

# Backend
cd backend
python -m venv venv
source venv/bin/activate
pip install -r requirements.txt
alembic upgrade head
uvicorn main:app --reload --port 8000

# Frontend (new terminal tab)
cd frontend
npm install
npm run dev
# → http://localhost:5173

# Useful
alembic revision --autogenerate -m "description"
alembic upgrade head
open http://localhost:8000/docs
```

**For the live "what's shipped / what's pending / what needs the user" status, see
[docs/CONTINUE-HERE.md](docs/CONTINUE-HERE.md) — that doc is kept current every session.**
This section only records the *initial* build milestone; the Decisions Log above is the
full, dated history of everything shipped since (Ally missions/skills/recipes/memory,
proactive fleet intelligence + threat monitoring, the Assets/Categories model with 5 cloud
providers, RDP foundation, and more).

<details>
<summary>Historical — initial-build status note, 2026-06-23 (superseded by CONTINUE-HERE.md)</summary>

Every build phase (0–13, incl. 2B) is done. ServerAlly is feature-complete:
auth, Linux SSH + Windows WinRM + hosting-panel management, AI chat/terminal,
playbooks, AI script generator, scheduler, file manager, monitoring & alerts,
security audit, backups, team management with role enforcement, and a production
deployment story (see DEPLOY.md).

**What's left is operational, not build work:**
- Run `DEPLOY.md` against a real VPS + managed Postgres/Redis.
- Validate the WinRM handshake against a live Windows Server, and the hosting
  adapters against a live CyberPanel/cPanel/Plesk (endpoints follow documented
  APIs but were only mock-tested).
- Consider the **Future Features Backlog** above for what to build next.

**Post-launch additions (2026-06-23):**
- **Dashboard, Activity Log, Settings** pages built (replaced placeholders); new `GET /api/activity` (AI commands + playbook-run feed) and an `access_info` column on `playbooks` (migration 010).
- **Playbook execution hardened** — live stderr streaming + non-zero-exit detection; the run modal shows live output, an ETA bar, and a post-run access card (URL/login).
- **Docker-based playbooks** self-install Docker via an `ensure_docker` preamble (idempotent).
- **Control-panel playbooks** added (free: CyberPanel/HestiaCP/aaPanel/CloudPanel; premium: cPanel/Plesk/DirectAdmin) behind a pre-flight guard — see the Script Library. **Need live validation on a fresh VPS** (official installers; CyberPanel's is piped-answer/version-sensitive).
- **Local dev** moved to backend :8888 / frontend :5190, Vite proxy on 127.0.0.1 (see OPS.md).

</details>

When starting new work, read this entire file first and keep the phase
checklists + Decisions Log up to date. Never deviate from the tech stack without
updating this file first.
